## url_feed.py

Reads the latest items from a site's RSS or Atom feed. Give it the feed's URL or just a page of the site: for a page, it finds the feed the page advertises (`<link rel="alternate" type="application/rss+xml">` or `atom+xml`), the way feed readers do. RSS 2.0, RSS 1.0 (RDF) and Atom are parsed into one item shape (title, link, published date, author, plain-text summary) and listed newest first. No API key required.

### Requirements

- Python 3.7+
- [`requests`](https://pypi.org/project/requests/), [`defusedxml`](https://pypi.org/project/defusedxml/) (safe XML parsing) and [`beautifulsoup4`](https://pypi.org/project/beautifulsoup4/) (finding a feed on a page)
- the repo's own `robots.py`, `hn.py` (for `clean_text()`) and `cli_utils.py` (run from the repo root)

```bash
pip install requests defusedxml beautifulsoup4
```

### Usage

```bash
python3 url_feed.py URL [--limit N] [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--max-chars N] [--respect-robots | --warn-robots] [--timeout SECONDS] [--json]
```

| Flag | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `URL` (positional) | — | A feed, or a page that links to its feed; must start with `http://` or `https://` |
| `--limit N` | 10 | Most items to show |
| `--since` | — | Only items published on or after this date (undated items are left out and counted) |
| `--max-chars N` | 300 | Longest summary per item |
| `--respect-robots` | off | Check robots.txt for the page and a discovered feed first, and refuse one that is disallowed or opts out of AI input; for feeds an agent chose |
| `--warn-robots` | off | Read regardless, but report what the feed's robots.txt asks in `ai_input_warning` / `robots_disallow_note`; for feeds the user chose |
| `--timeout SECONDS` | 10 | Request timeout |
| `--json` | off | Print machine-readable JSON to stdout instead of a human-readable report |

#### Example output

From a live run on 2026-10-21:

```
$ python3 url_feed.py https://blog.python.org --limit 3
Reading the feed for: https://blog.python.org

Feed: Python Insider (RSS)
  https://blog.python.org/rss.xml
  found on https://blog.python.org

Showing 3 of 50 item(s), newest first

1. Python 3.16.0 alpha 1 is now available
   2026-10-13 00:00:00 UTC - Savannah Ostrowski
   https://blog.python.org/2026/10/python-3160-alpha-1-is-now-available/
   The first developer preview of Python 3.16 is ready for testing.

2. Python 3.15.0 (final) is here!
   2026-10-09 00:00:00 UTC - Hugo van Kemenade
   https://blog.python.org/2026/10/python-3150-final-is-here/
   A brand new Python here for you to enjoy!

3. Python 3.15.0 candidate 3 is here!
   2026-10-02 00:00:00 UTC - Hugo van Kemenade
   https://blog.python.org/2026/10/python-3150-rc3/
   Following tradition, a surprise rc3!
```

#### Tool usage (`--json`)

```json
{
  "url": "https://github.com/python/cpython/releases.atom",
  "feed_url": "https://github.com/python/cpython/releases.atom",
  "discovered": false,
  "format": "atom",
  "title": "Release notes from cpython",
  "site_url": "https://github.com/python/cpython/releases",
  "item_count": 10,
  "undated_skipped": 0,
  "items": [
    {"title": "v3.16.0a1", "link": "https://github.com/python/cpython/releases/tag/v3.16.0a1", "published": "2026-10-13T15:05:54Z", "author": "savannahostrowski", "summary": "Python 3.16.0a1"}
  ],
  "robots_checked": false,
  "ai_input_warning": null,
  "robots_disallow_note": null
}
```

(From a live run on 2026-10-21 with `--limit 1`.) `published` is an ISO 8601 UTC time, or `null` when the feed gives no readable date.

### How it works

1. **Fetch** the URL with this script's own User-Agent (`online-search-url-feed`), refusing a body over 5 MB. With `--respect-robots`, `robots.check()` runs first.
2. **Feed or page?** (`looks_like_feed()`): an HTML or XHTML Content-Type is a page; an RSS, Atom or other XML type is a feed; otherwise the body's start (`<?xml`, `<rss`, `<feed`) decides.
3. **Discover** (`discover_feed()`): for a page, the first `<link rel="alternate">` with an RSS or Atom type, resolved against the page's URL, is fetched (after its own robots check with `--respect-robots`). A page with none is an error; the script never scrapes a page instead.
4. **Parse** (`parse_feed()`) with `defusedxml`, which refuses entity-expansion ("billion laughs") and external-entity tricks. Atom entries take `published`, else `updated`; RSS items take `pubDate`, else `dc:date`; summaries come from `description` / `summary`, else the full content, through `hn.clean_text()`. `parse_date()` reads RFC 822 and ISO 8601 dates into UTC and leaves anything else `null`.
5. **Select** (`select_items()`): dated items newest first, undated ones after them in feed order; `--since` drops older and undated items; then `--limit`.
6. **Report**, with `--warn-robots` notes from `robots.review()` on the feed actually read.

### API reference

- `read_feed(url, limit, since, max_chars, timeout, robots_check=None)` — the whole run, returning the JSON-ready dict; `robots_check(url)` is called on the page and on a discovered feed.
- `fetch(url, timeout)`, `looks_like_feed(content_type, body)`, `discover_feed(html, base_url)` — fetching and finding the feed.
- `parse_feed(body, max_chars)` — `{format, title, site_url, items}`; raises `NotAFeed` (a `ValueError`) for XML that isn't RSS or Atom, `ValueError` for malformed or unsafe XML.
- `parse_date(value)`, `select_items(items, limit, since)`, `format_result(result)`.

### Error handling

- A missing scheme, a network error or 4xx/5xx, a page with no feed, XML that isn't a feed, malformed or unsafe XML, a body over 5 MB, a robots refusal (`blocked: …`) or a missing `defusedxml` / `beautifulsoup4` exits with status 1: `Error: …` on stderr, or `{"error": "..."}` with `--json`.
- Invalid `--limit`, `--since`, `--max-chars` or `--timeout` values, or both robots flags at once, exit with status 2 before any request.

### Notes / limitations

- Feeds usually hold only the latest 10–50 items, so `--since` can't reach further back than the feed does.
- A summary is the publisher's own blurb (or the start of the full text), cut to `--max-chars`; it isn't a summary of the article.
- Only the first advertised feed of a page is read; a site with several (posts, comments, categories) may need the specific feed URL.
- JSON Feed (`application/feed+json`) isn't supported.
- With `--warn-robots`, some feed hosts ask automated clients not to fetch them (on 2026-10-21, `export.arxiv.org`'s robots.txt disallowed its `/rss/` feeds); the note says so.

---

## Claude Code agent: `url-feed`

A [Claude Code](https://claude.com/claude-code) subagent definition at `.claude/agents/url-feed.md`. It calls no API itself — it shells out to [`url_feed.py`](#url_feedpy) via the `Bash` tool and reports the result conversationally.

### Purpose

Lets Claude Code answer "what's new on the Python blog?", "latest releases of cpython", "newest arXiv cs.CL papers" or "what has this blog published since October 1st?" from the site's own feed.

### How it's invoked

- **Automatically** — Claude Code selects it when the user wants a site's recent posts, articles, releases or episodes, given a feed URL, a site URL or just a site's name. One page's content stays with `url-content`, summaries with `url-summary`, and a page's links with `url-links`.
- **Explicitly** — via the `Agent` tool with `subagent_type: "url-feed"`.

Subagent definitions are loaded when a Claude Code session starts, so a newly added or edited agent file only takes effect in sessions started afterward — not the session it was created in.

### What it does

1. Uses the URL the user gave (adding `https://` if needed) with `--warn-robots`. If the user only named a site, it picks the URL itself, says which, and passes `--respect-robots`, following the repo's rule for agent-chosen URLs.
2. Runs `python3 url_feed.py "URL" --json` with `--limit` / `--since` from the request.
3. Lists the items newest first (title as a link, readable date, author, one-line gist), with a one-line robots note when the tool reports one, and says when nothing falls in a `--since` window or the feed doesn't reach back that far.

### Configuration

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| `tools` | `Bash` |
| Underlying script | [`url_feed.py`](#url_feedpy) |

### Notes / limitations

- Requires `url_feed.py` and its dependencies (`requests`, `defusedxml`, `beautifulsoup4`) to be runnable from the repo root.
- Never scrapes a site that has no feed; it says so instead. To summarize posts, it points to the `url-summary` agent.
- Being a prompt rather than code, the agent isn't covered by the offline test suite. [`docs/features/agent/url-feed/test-runs.md`](docs/features/agent/url-feed/test-runs.md) lists targeted live runs of its judgement rules (a user-given site, a named site, a robots note, a date window beyond the feed, no feed, a robots refusal); all passed on 2026-10-21, and the `--since` run led to raising `--limit` with a date window.
- Inherits all the limitations of [`url_feed.py`](#url_feedpy) itself (short feed history, first advertised feed only, no JSON Feed).

---

## url_metadata.py

Retrieves a web page at a given URL and extracts its metadata: the `<title>`, the meta description, the canonical URL, and the OpenGraph (`og:*`) and Twitter-card (`twitter:*`) tags that sites use to build link previews. Makes an HTTP GET request and parses the HTML with BeautifulSoup. The canonical URL and URL-valued tags are resolved against the page's final URL (after redirects). No API key required.

### Requirements

- `requests`
- `beautifulsoup4`
- the repo's own `cli_utils.py` module (no install — run from the repo root so it imports)

### Usage

```bash
python3 url_metadata.py URL [--timeout SECONDS] [--json]
```

| Flag | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `URL` (positional) | — | URL to fetch, e.g. `https://example.com`; must start with `http://` or `https://` |
| `--timeout SECONDS` | 10 | Request timeout in seconds |
| `--json` | off | Print machine-readable JSON to stdout instead of a human-readable report — for calling this script as a tool from an agent or another program |

#### Example output

```
Fetching: https://github.com/anthropics

URL: https://github.com/anthropics
Status: 200

Title:       Anthropic · GitHub
Description: Anthropic has 115 repositories available. Follow their code on GitHub.
Canonical:   https://github.com/anthropics

OpenGraph:
  image        https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/76263028?s=280&v=4
  image:alt    Anthropic has 115 repositories available. Follow their code on GitHub.
  site_name    GitHub
  type         profile
  title        Anthropic
  url          https://github.com/anthropics
  description  Anthropic has 115 repositories available. Follow their code on GitHub.

Twitter card:
  image        https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/76263028?s=280&v=4
  site         @github
  card         summary_large_image
  title        Anthropic
  description  Anthropic has 115 repositories available. Follow their code on GitHub.
```

A page without them reports `-` for a missing title / description / canonical URL and `none` for an empty tag group (`https://example.com` has only a title).

#### Tool usage (`--json`)

```bash
python3 url_metadata.py https://github.com/anthropics --json
```

```json
{
  "url": "https://github.com/anthropics",
  "final_url": "https://github.com/anthropics",
  "status_code": 200,
  "content_type": "text/html; charset=utf-8",
  "title": "Anthropic · GitHub",
  "description": "Anthropic has 115 repositories available. Follow their code on GitHub.",
  "canonical": "https://github.com/anthropics",
  "open_graph": {
    "image": "https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/76263028?s=280&v=4",
    "site_name": "GitHub",
    "type": "profile",
    "title": "Anthropic",
    "url": "https://github.com/anthropics"
  },
  "twitter": {
    "site": "@github",
    "card": "summary_large_image",
    "title": "Anthropic"
  }
}
```

(Trimmed.) `open_graph` and `twitter` keep each tag's name without its prefix, in page order. A missing `title`, `description` or `canonical` is `null`, and a page with no tags in a group gives `{}`. Non-ASCII characters are `\u`-escaped, as in the other `url_*.py` scripts. With `--json`, the leading progress line is suppressed and the result prints as a single JSON object on stdout. On failure, an exit code of `1` is returned and a JSON object (`{"error": "..."}`) is printed to stderr instead of plain text.

### How it works

1. **Validate the URL** — Rejects a URL that doesn't start with `http://` or `https://` before making any request.
2. **Fetch** — Issues a `GET` with a browser-like `User-Agent`, following redirects.
3. **Check the response** — A `4xx`/`5xx` status raises `requests.HTTPError`; a `Content-Type` that doesn't contain `html` raises `ValueError`.
4. **Extract** — Parses the body with BeautifulSoup (`html.parser`):
   - **Title** — the `<title>` inside `<head>` (an inline `<svg>` can carry its own `<title>`), falling back to the first `<title>` anywhere; whitespace collapsed.
   - **Meta tags** — each `<meta>` keyed by its `property` or `name` attribute, lower-cased (sites use both attributes for both groups). `description` fills the description; `og:*` and `twitter:*` fill their groups with the prefix stripped. A tag with empty content is skipped, and a repeated tag keeps its first value. URL-valued keys (`url`, `image`, `image:url`, `image:secure_url`) are resolved with `urllib.parse.urljoin`.
   - **Canonical** — the first `<link>` whose `rel` includes `canonical` (case-insensitive) and has a non-empty `href`, resolved against the final URL.

### Configuration

These are set as constants near the top of the file — edit them directly to change behavior:

| Constant | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `TIMEOUT_SECONDS` | 10 | Default request timeout, used unless `--timeout` overrides it |
| `USER_AGENT` | `Mozilla/5.0 (compatible; online-search-url-metadata/1.0)` | `User-Agent` header sent with the request — some sites 403 requests with no/default UA |
| `TAG_GROUPS` | `{"open_graph": "og:", "twitter": "twitter:"}` | Output key and meta-name prefix of each tag group |
| `URL_KEYS` | `url`, `image`, `image:url`, `image:secure_url` | Tag names (without prefix) whose values are resolved to absolute URLs |

### API reference

- `extract_metadata(html, base_url)` — Parses `html` and returns `{title, description, canonical, open_graph, twitter}`, resolving URLs against `base_url`. Raises `ImportError` if `beautifulsoup4` isn't installed (imported lazily, so the rest of the script works without it).
- `fetch_metadata(url, timeout=TIMEOUT_SECONDS)` — Issues the `GET` request and returns `{url, final_url, status_code, content_type}` plus the `extract_metadata()` fields. Raises `requests.HTTPError` on a 4xx/5xx response, `ValueError` on a non-HTML `Content-Type`, and `ImportError` if `beautifulsoup4` isn't installed; connection-level failures propagate as `requests.RequestException` for the caller to handle.
- `format_result(result)` — Renders a `fetch_metadata()` record as a multi-line human-readable string, including a "Redirected to:" line when `final_url` differs from `url`, `-` for a missing field and `none` for an empty tag group.
- `parse_args(argv=None)` — Parses the `url` positional and `--timeout` / `--json` CLI flags.
- `main(argv=None)` — Entry point; validates the URL scheme, calls `fetch_metadata()`, handles top-level errors, and prints the result (either human-readable or JSON, depending on `--json`).

### Error handling

- If the URL doesn't start with `http://` or `https://`, the script prints an error and exits with status code 1 before making any request.
- If the server returns a `4xx`/`5xx` status, the script prints an error (`requests`' own `HTTPError` message) and exits with status code 1.
- If the response's `Content-Type` doesn't contain `html`, the script prints an error (`"URL did not return HTML content (Content-Type: ...)"`) and exits with status code 1.
- If `beautifulsoup4` isn't installed, the script prints `"beautifulsoup4 is required (pip install beautifulsoup4)"` and exits with status code 1.
- If the request fails at the connection level (DNS failure, timeout, connection refused, etc.), the script prints an error and exits with status code 1 — plain text on stderr normally, or a JSON object (`{"error": "..."}`) on stderr when `--json` is passed.
- An out-of-range `--timeout` (e.g. `0` or negative) is rejected by argparse before any request, exiting with status code 2.

### Notes / limitations

- No JavaScript rendering — single-page apps that set their title or tags client-side can come back with `null` / `{}` even though a browser shows them.
- A repeated tag keeps only its first value, so a page with several `og:image` tags reports just the first.
- Other metadata (`<html lang>`, favicons, JSON-LD structured data, `<meta name="robots">`, Dublin Core tags) isn't extracted.
- Content-type sniffing is a simple substring check (`"html" in content_type.lower()`) on the `Content-Type` header, not a body sniff.
- Some sites block automated requests regardless of `User-Agent` (bot-detection, Cloudflare challenges, etc.), which can surface as a `4xx`/`5xx` error even though the page is reachable in a real browser.
- A single attempt, no retry — a transient network hiccup is reported as a connection-level error rather than retried internally.

---

## Claude Code agent: `url-metadata`

A [Claude Code](https://claude.com/claude-code) subagent definition at `.claude/agents/url-metadata.md`. It calls no API itself — it shells out to [`url_metadata.py`](#url_metadatapy) via the `Bash` tool and reports the result conversationally.

### Purpose

Lets Claude Code answer questions like "what's the title of this page?", "what will this link look like when I share it?", "get the OpenGraph tags for this URL", "what's the canonical URL of this page?" or "label these links with their page titles" by running the script and reporting its JSON output, instead of guessing.

### How it's invoked

- **Automatically** — Claude Code selects this subagent on its own when the user gives one or more URLs and wants their title, description, canonical URL or social-preview tags, based on the `description` field in its frontmatter. The page's full content goes to [`url-content`](#claude-code-agent-url-content), a summary to [`url-summary`](#claude-code-agent-url-summary), its links to [`url-links`](#claude-code-agent-url-links), and reachability to [`url-availability`](#claude-code-agent-url-availability) instead.
- **Explicitly** — via the `Agent` tool with `subagent_type: "url-metadata"`.

Subagent definitions are loaded when a Claude Code session starts, so a newly added or edited agent file only takes effect in sessions started afterward — not the session it was created in.

### What it does

1. Adds a `https://` scheme itself if the user gave a bare domain or path (the underlying script requires one).
2. Runs `python3 url_metadata.py "URL" --json` from the repo root, once per URL.
3. Leads with what the user asked for (e.g. just the title), otherwise gives the title, description and canonical URL, then the preview tags. For a link-preview question it says which values a preview would use (`og:*`, falling back to the title and description) and what's missing, such as an `og:image`. It notes a canonical URL that differs from the final URL, and a redirect. Several URLs go in one table, with any failures and their reasons.
4. Distinguishes an HTTP error, a non-HTML response, a missing `beautifulsoup4` dependency, and a connection-level failure (all surfaced as `{"error": "..."}`) when reporting a failure, and retries a connection-level failure at most once when it looks transient.

### Configuration

The agent's frontmatter restricts it to the `Bash` tool only, since running the script and reading its stdout is all it needs.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| `tools` | `Bash` |
| Underlying script | [`url_metadata.py`](#url_metadatapy) |

### Notes / limitations

- Requires `url_metadata.py` (and its `requests`/`beautifulsoup4` dependencies) to be present and runnable from the repo root.
- Its description of how a preview card would look follows the usual `og:*` / `twitter:*` conventions; each social site has its own rules and caches, so the real card can differ.
- Inherits all the limitations of [`url_metadata.py`](#url_metadatapy) itself (no JavaScript rendering, first value only for a repeated tag, no retry inside the script, bot-detection/Cloudflare challenges can surface as a false error).

---

## Claude Code agent: `url-summary`

A [Claude Code](https://claude.com/claude-code) subagent definition at `.claude/agents/url-summary.md`. It calls no API itself — it shells out to [`url_content.py`](#url_contentpy) via the `Bash` tool, once per URL it's given, and reports a summary conversationally. Unlike [`url-content`](#claude-code-agent-url-content), it always summarizes rather than quoting/reporting raw content, and it accepts one *or more* URLs in a single request rather than just one.

### Purpose

Lets Claude Code answer requests like "summarize this article: https://example.com/post", "summarize these URLs for me", or "what do these three pages say, in short" — fetching each page and reporting what it actually says, instead of guessing from the URL or title alone.

### How it's invoked

- **Automatically** — Claude Code selects this subagent on its own when the user gives one or more URLs and wants a summary of their content, based on the `description` field in its frontmatter.
- **Explicitly** — via the `Agent` tool with `subagent_type: "url-summary"`.

Subagent definitions are loaded when a Claude Code session starts, so a newly added or edited agent file only takes effect in sessions started afterward — not the session it was created in.

### What it does

1. Adds a `https://` scheme itself to any URL the user gave as a bare domain or path (the underlying script requires one).
2. Runs `python3 url_content.py "URL" --text --max-chars 6000 --json` for *every* URL given, one call per URL — `--text` for readable content rather than raw markup, and a smaller `--max-chars` than `url-content`'s default since this agent summarizes rather than reproduces the page.
3. For each URL that fetched successfully, writes a concise summary of its actual content (not a full recap), noting if `truncated` is `true`.
4. Labels each summary with the page's `title` (from `url_content.py --text`) and its URL, or the URL alone when the page has no title. For a single URL, reports that one summary. For multiple URLs, reports each summary individually, adding a short combined take only when the pages are genuinely related — never forcing a synthesis across unrelated pages.
5. Reports every URL given, even ones that failed to fetch — a failed URL is shown with its error reason alongside the successful summaries, never silently dropped. Retries a connection-level failure at most once when it looks transient; a missing-scheme, non-textual-content, or missing-dependency failure is not retried, since none of those change on retry.

### Configuration

The agent's frontmatter restricts it to the `Bash` tool only, since running the script and reading its stdout is all it needs.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| `tools` | `Bash` |
| Underlying script | [`url_content.py`](#url_contentpy) (called once per URL) |

### Notes / limitations

- Passes `--warn-robots` on every fetch: the user chose the pages, so each is always fetched and summarized, but a page whose site opts out of AI input (`Content-Signal: ai-input=no`) or whose robots.txt asks automated clients not to fetch it gets a one-line note under its label.
- Requires `url_content.py` and the `url_metadata.py` / `cli_utils.py` modules it imports (and its `requests`/`beautifulsoup4` dependencies) to be present and runnable from the repo root.
- Fetches each URL's initial HTML response only — no JavaScript rendering, so a JavaScript-heavy page may summarize as little more than its shell.
- `--text` extraction is blunt (drops script/style, flattens the rest to one line, no paragraph breaks) — the agent summarizes from that flattened text, so nav/footer/cookie-banner text mixed into the extracted content can occasionally bleed into a summary.
- The combined "how these pages relate" take, when given multiple URLs, is a judgment call made by the agent itself, not a deterministic script — it's included only when a real relationship exists, and omitted rather than invented for unrelated pages.
- Sequential, one URL at a time — summarizing many URLs in one request means that many separate network round trips, not a single batched call.
- Being a prompt rather than code, the agent isn't covered by the offline test suite. [`docs/features/agent/url-summary/test-runs.md`](docs/features/agent/url-summary/test-runs.md) lists targeted live runs of its judgement rules (adding `https://`, truncation, a page with no title, the robots.txt disallow note, failed URLs kept in the report, a combined take only for related pages); all passed on 2026-10-21. The `ai-input=no` note is untested for want of a live site that sets it for every client.
- Inherits all the limitations of [`url_content.py`](#url_contentpy) itself (no retry inside the script, bot-detection/Cloudflare challenges can surface as a false error, single-attempt GET, hard character-cutoff truncation).

# Development

---

## Tests

Unit tests live in [`tests/`](tests/) and run **offline** — every network call is monkeypatched, so no API is hit.

```bash
pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
python3 -m pytest
```

Coverage spans the pure logic (`indicators.py`, `valuation.py`, `yahoo_finance.py` parsing and window flags, `cli_utils.py`), each script's argument parser and output formatters, the Yahoo cookie/crumb handshake and `quoteSummary` fetch (`yahoo_finance.py`, `market_top_volume.py`, `stock_intrinsic_value.py`), and the aggregators (`get_movers`, `get_hottest_tech_discussions`, `find_stock_buzz`, …) with their HTTP seams stubbed. The doctests in `indicators.py` and `valuation.py` are run too.

[`tests/README.md`](tests/README.md) documents the layout, the `FakeResponse` / `FakeSession` doubles and `chart_result` fixture, the monkeypatching conventions, a per-module coverage table, and how to add a test.

---

## cli_utils.py

Small helpers shared by the command-line scripts: UTF-8 output, uniform error exits, URL-scheme validation, integer-argument validation, `.env` loading, and the result-list formatting/printing shared by the six `web_search_*.py` scripts that return a flat `[{rank, title, url, snippet}, ...]` array (`web_search_perplexity.py`'s different answer-plus-sources shape doesn't use these last two). Not a CLI — it is imported, not run.

### Requirements

- Python 3.7+ (standard library only)

### API reference

- `use_utf8_output()` — Switches `sys.stdout` and `sys.stderr` to UTF-8. Every script's `main()` calls it first, before parsing arguments. Without it, Python on Windows writes piped or redirected output in the ANSI code page (cp1252): `°` and similar characters garble in a UTF-8 reader such as Git Bash, and any character cp1252 lacks (Japanese, Chinese, emoji, many symbols in web-search results or HN comments) raises `UnicodeEncodeError` and crashes the script. A stream that can't be reconfigured (e.g. one replaced by a test harness) is left alone. `--json` output was never affected, since `json.dumps` escapes non-ASCII characters.
- `die(message, as_json=False)` — Prints an error to stderr and exits the process with status code 1. With `as_json=True` the error is emitted as `{"error": message}` (for the `--json` tool modes); otherwise as a plain `Error: <message>` line. Used everywhere the scripts previously hand-rolled the same "print and `sys.exit(1)`" block.
- `redact(message, secret)` — `message` with every occurrence of `secret` replaced by `<redacted>`; the keyed `web_search_*.py` scripts pass their error messages through it.
- `http_error_detail(response)` — The provider's own reason for a failed request: a JSON body's `error` / `message` / `detail` (a string, a `{code, detail, message}` object as Brave sends, or a list of validation errors), else a short non-HTML text body, else `None`.
- `raise_for_status(response)` — `response.raise_for_status()`, but when `http_error_detail()` finds a reason, the same exception type is raised as `HTTP <status>: <reason>` instead of requests' bare `422 Client Error: … for url: …`. Used by the keyed `web_search_*.py` scripts.
- `require_env(name, script, as_json=False)` — Returns environment variable `name`, or calls `die()` (status code 1) with `"<name> environment variable must be set. See the module docstring in <script> for how to obtain one."` if it is unset or empty. Used by the six `web_search_*.py` scripts that need an API key, each calling `load_dotenv()` first so a `.env` value counts.
- `validate_url_scheme(url, as_json=False)` — Calls `die()` (status code 1) unless `url` starts with `http://` or `https://`. Shared by the four `url_*.py` scripts (`url_content.py`, `url_availability.py`, `url_links.py`, `url_metadata.py`), which all take a URL positional and reject a bare domain/path before making any request.
- `response_text(response)` — A `requests` response's body as text, decoded correctly. When the `Content-Type` header names no charset, `requests` falls back to ISO-8859-1 for `text/*`, which garbles UTF-8 pages ("—" comes out as "â€”"). In that case this uses the encoding `requests` detects from the bytes; a charset the server names is left alone. Used by `url_content.py`, `url_links.py` and `url_metadata.py` (not `url_availability.py`, which never reads the body).
- `positive_int(value)` — An argparse `type` callable for an integer that must be `1` or greater; raises `argparse.ArgumentTypeError` on a non-integer or a value `< 1`. Wired to `--last` (the four price scripts), `--years` (`stock_intrinsic_value.py`), `--timeout` / `--max-chars` (the `url_*.py` scripts), the result-count flags of all seven `web_search_*.py` scripts, `--limit` (`hottest_tech_discussions.py`, `market_top_volume.py` and `osv_vulnerabilities.py`), and `--stock-limit` / `--discussion-limit` (`stock_tech_buzz_agent.py`), so a bad value is rejected at parse time (exit code 2) rather than after the request.
- `non_negative_int(value)` — Like `positive_int`, but `0` is allowed; for options where 0 means "none" or "no limit": `--replies` / `--max-chars` (`hn_thread.py`) and `hn_search.py`'s `--max-chars` and `--min-points`.
- `bounded_int(maximum)` — Returns an argparse `type` callable for an integer from `1` to `maximum` (rejected as `positive_int` does, or with `must be at most <maximum>`); wired to `--top` / `--depth` in `hn_thread.py`.
- `load_dotenv(path=ENV_FILE)` — Populates `os.environ` from a simple `KEY=VALUE` `.env` file (`ENV_FILE` defaults to `.env` next to `cli_utils.py`, i.e. the repo root, the same directory every script importing it lives in); missing file is silently ignored; never overwrites an already-set environment variable, so real env vars always take precedence over `.env` values. Used by every `web_search_*.py` script that needs an API key (all but `web_search_duckduckgo.py`, which needs no key).
- `format_result(row)` — Renders a `{rank, title, url, snippet}` record (the shape every `web_search_*.py` script but `web_search_perplexity.py` produces) as a multi-line human-readable string.
- `print_results(rows, as_json)` — Prints a list of `{rank, title, url, snippet}` records: the list as JSON with `as_json=True`; otherwise `"No results found."` for an empty list, or each row through `format_result()` separated by a blank line. This is the shared tail of each of those scripts' `main()`.

### Notes / limitations

- `die()` always exits `1`; the argparse layer's own errors (an unparsable value, a rejected `positive_int`) exit `2`. Scripts that had a manual `--last < 1` / `--years < 1` check now get that rejection from argparse instead, so those cases changed from exit `1` to exit `2` and now happen before any network call.
- `load_dotenv()`'s `.env` parsing is intentionally minimal (`KEY=VALUE` lines, optional quoting, `#` comments) — it doesn't handle multi-line values or shell-style variable expansion.
- `format_result()` / `print_results()` assume the `{rank, title, url, snippet}` shape; they aren't used by `web_search_perplexity.py`, whose Sonar-based output shape is a synthesized answer plus a source list rather than a flat results array (see that script's own section for why).

---

## robots.py

Shared helper behind [`url_content.py`](#url_contentpy)'s `--respect-robots` and `--warn-robots`: may this client fetch a page, and may an AI use it? It reads a site's `robots.txt` by the rules of [RFC 9309](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9309), plus its `Content-Signal` lines ([contentsignals.org](https://contentsignals.org/)), e.g. `Content-Signal: search=yes, ai-input=no`. Not a CLI — it is imported, not run. Added after the [source audit](docs/source-audit.md) found that agents fetched pages they had chosen themselves without asking.

Python's own `urllib.robotparser` isn't used: it takes the *first* matching rule rather than the longest, so `Allow: /` before `Disallow: /search` would allow `/search`, and it ignores `Content-Signal`.

### Requirements

- Python 3.7+
- [`requests`](https://pypi.org/project/requests/)

### How it decides

1. **Fetch** `/robots.txt` from the page's site with the caller's `User-Agent`, following redirects, reading at most 500 KiB. As RFC 9309 says, a `4xx` (no file) allows everything, while a `5xx` or a connection failure means nothing can be assumed allowed.
2. **Pick the groups** — the `User-agent` groups naming the caller's product token (case-insensitively, e.g. `online-search-url-content`), or, if none do, the `*` groups. Consecutive `User-agent` lines share one group.
3. **Match the rules** — `Allow` / `Disallow` patterns against the page's path and query, with `*` matching any run of characters and a final `$` anchoring the end. The longest matching pattern wins, and `Allow` wins a tie. `/robots.txt` itself is always allowed.
4. **Read the signal** — the `Content-Signal` lines in the chosen groups, plus any before the first group (which apply to everyone). A path-scoped signal (`Content-Signal: /docs/ ai-input=yes`) beats an unscoped one, and a longer path a shorter one. Only `ai-input` is acted on: it covers feeding a page into an AI model to answer a question or write a summary, which is what this repo's agents do with fetched pages. No `ai-input` at all means no stated preference.

### API reference

- `check(url, user_agent, token, timeout=TIMEOUT_SECONDS)` — `None` if the page may be fetched and given to an AI, otherwise the reason it may not, e.g. `"example.com's robots.txt opts out of AI input (Content-Signal: ai-input=no)"`, `"example.com's robots.txt disallows /search for <token>"` or `"example.com: its robots.txt returned HTTP 503, which counts as a full disallow"`.
- `review(url, user_agent, token, timeout=TIMEOUT_SECONDS)` — For `--warn-robots`: what the robots.txt asks, as notes rather than a verdict, from one fetch. Returns `{"ai_input": reason or None, "disallowed": reason or None}`: the first if the site opts out of AI input (`Content-Signal: ai-input=no`), the second if a rule asks automated clients not to fetch the page (`"example.com's robots.txt asks automated clients not to fetch /path"`). An unreadable robots.txt gives `None` for both, since no preference is known.
- `fetch(url, user_agent, timeout=TIMEOUT_SECONDS)` — `(text, problem)` for the site's robots.txt (step 1).
- `parse(text)` — `(groups, global_signals)`; each group is `{"agents", "rules": [(allow, pattern)], "signals": [(path_or_None, {key: value})]}`.
- `parse_signal(value)` — One `Content-Signal` value as `(path_or_None, {key: value})`, lowercased.
- `matching_groups(groups, token)`, `is_allowed(groups, token, path)`, `ai_input_allowed(groups, global_signals, token, path)` — Steps 2–4.
- `path_matches(pattern, path)`, `url_path(url)`, `robots_url(url)` — Pattern matching, the path-plus-query a rule is matched against (`/` if empty, no fragment), and the site's robots.txt URL.

### Configuration

| Constant | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `TIMEOUT_SECONDS` | `10` | Timeout for the robots.txt request |
| `MAX_BYTES` | `500 * 1024` | How much of a robots.txt is read (RFC 9309's minimum) |

### Notes / limitations

- One robots.txt request per check: `url_content.py` runs once per page, so nothing is cached between pages of one site.
- Paths are matched as sent, without percent-decoding them first; a rule and a URL that spell one character differently (`%7E` vs `~`) don't match.
- `Content-Signal` is a newer, voluntary convention. Its `search` and `ai-train` keys are parsed but not acted on: the repo neither builds a search index nor trains models.
- Like robots.txt itself, this is a request the site makes, not access control: it covers sites that state a preference this way, not ones that say so only in their terms.
